Applying its logarithmic identity conditionally on the root data to marks $mV_\alpha$ shows that the expectation of the divided logarithm on the right of <a href="#ct:natural-average" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ct:natural-average">[ct:natural-average]</a> is the nested transform with hidden exponents $mx_1,\ldots,mx_h$, followed by the sample exponent $m$. The scaling is the identity $$T_x(mV)=mT_{mx}V,$$ applied successively at the hidden levels and followed by division by $m$. Below them remain the old exponents $t_2,\ldots,t_d$. This proves precisely <a href="#ct:new-exponents" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ct:new-exponents">[ct:new-exponents]</a>. The hidden exponents, when present, are increasing and lie strictly between zero and $m$. Every old descendant exponent is at least $m$, and their old order persists. Thus these are admissible intermediate trial exponents, including when some old exponents coincide or equal one.

Finally remove the conditioning on counts and signs. Both the prelimit and limit transforms are bounded in absolute value by $k\log2+\beta\sum D_i$, with the fixed Poisson packet laws. Their tails are uniformly integrable. Truncating counts, applying the proved finite-count assertion, and then removing the truncation establishes the assertion for each expected endpoint. Subtracting the B endpoint from the A endpoint and using <a href="#ct:selected-value" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ct:selected-value">[ct:selected-value]</a> yields $G_k/k\le U$. ◻

</details>

<a id="ct:sub-records"></a>
#### Passing the pathwise structural records

The endpoint calculation has supplied a trial with the required value bound. It remains to preserve the old records and to construct the new one. We will pass empirical products of site tests through the weak limit; the following Hilbert-space fact then converts those pairwise bounds into statements about almost every individual global path.

<a id="stmt-ct-support"></a>
<div id="ct:support" class="lemma">

**Lemma 24** (Closed support bounds). *Let $V,V'$ be independent random variables with the same law in a separable Hilbert space, and let $\delta,\varepsilon\ge0$.*

1.  *If $|\langle V,V'\rangle|\le\delta^2$ almost surely, then $\|V\|\le\delta$ almost surely.*

2.  *If $\langle V,V'\rangle$ almost surely belongs to a fixed closed interval of length $\varepsilon$, then the support of their common law has diameter at most $\sqrt{2\varepsilon}$. Consequently $\|V-\mathbb EV\|\le\sqrt{2\varepsilon}$ almost surely whenever the expectation exists.*

*If $V,V'$ are conditionally independent with the same conditional law given a standard Borel random element, the corresponding conclusions hold for almost every conditional law in which the stated pair bound holds.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-ct-support"><summary>Proof of stmt-ct-support</summary>

*Proof.* A closed set of full product measure contains the product of the two supports: otherwise a pair of support points has product neighborhoods of positive measure disjoint from that set. The first assertion follows by putting the same support point in both coordinates. In the second, for support points $v,v'$, both squared norms and their inner product belong to the same interval, so $$\|v-v'\|^2=\|v\|^2+\|v'\|^2-2\langle v,v'\rangle
                              \le2\varepsilon.$$ Jensen’s inequality gives the assertion about the barycenter. The same reasoning applies after disintegration; separability ensures that the relevant supports have full measure. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-ct-old-records"></a>
<div id="ct:old-records" class="lemma">

**Lemma 25** (Preservation of the old records). *Every old processed block satisfies Equation <a href="#ctr:record" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ctr:record">[ctr:record]</a> in the represented hierarchy, with the same test family and tolerance and with its boundaries shifted by $h$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-ct-old-records"><summary>Proof of stmt-ct-old-records</summary>

*Proof.* Let $(b,c]$ be an old block. Since the input hierarchy is active, $b\ge1$. In the prelimit natural array we may therefore choose two paths in the same first-level sample, agreeing through $b$ and diverging immediately afterward. At site $i$, form the product of their two values of $$\phi(S_c)-S_b(\phi),
                         \qquad \phi\in\Phi_j.$$ Condition on the complete globals of the two paths. The sites are independent and identically distributed, and the conditional mean of this product is the $L^2(\,\mathrm du_{0:b})$ inner product of the two error functions $$E_z(u_{0:b})=
          \int\phi(S_c)\,\,\mathrm du_{b+1:c}-S_b(\phi).$$ The fresh site variables after the branching point are independent on the two paths; their shared site variables are exactly those through $b$. Equation <a href="#ctr:record" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ctr:record">[ctr:record]</a>, which survived the posterior and Gaussian changes of law, bounds both error norms by $\delta$. Cauchy–Schwarz therefore bounds the absolute conditional mean by $\delta^2$.

Let $M_I$ be the empirical mean over the first $I$ sites. The summands belong to $[-1,1]$, so <a id="ct:old-empirical-bound"></a>$$\label{ct:old-empirical-bound}
     \mathbb E\operatorname{dist}(M_I,[-\delta^2,\delta^2])^2\le I^{-1}.$$ For fixed $I$, the integrand on its left is a bounded continuous function of finitely many retained type values: $\phi$ is continuous, and evaluation of a probability measure against $\phi$ is continuous in the type-space topology. The inequality thus passes to the limit. On $I=2^j$, Markov’s inequality and Borel–Cantelli imply that the displayed distance tends to zero almost surely. This holds simultaneously for all countably many path choices and all the finitely many tests. It consequently remains true for the partition-selected paths after reindexing.

In the product-tree representation, the conditional strong law for the sites identifies the limit of $M_I$ with the inner product of the analogous error vectors, now in $L^2(\,\mathrm du_{0:b+h})$. Conditional on the common globals through the shifted block start, those two vectors are independent and identically distributed as their continuation globals vary. Thus their inner product has absolute value at most $\delta^2$ almost surely. Apply the first assertion of Lemma <a href="#ct:support" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ct:support">24</a> to this conditional law. Each error vector has norm at most $\delta$ for almost every global continuation. Fubini’s theorem gives precisely Equation <a href="#ctr:record" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ctr:record">[ctr:record]</a> for almost every complete global path. The weak-limit step used a closed finite-column condition; the pathwise conclusion then followed from the conditional support bound. ◻

</details>

Thus every previously processed block retains its record. For the new block, the narrow support band will provide the corresponding pairwise bound without a preexisting record.

<a id="stmt-ct-new-record"></a>
<div id="ct:new-record" class="lemma">

**Lemma 26** (A new processed block). *Let $c=h+1$ be the sample level. Let $b=1$ if the parent cut is present, and let $b=0$ otherwise. There is an ancestor-measurable probability-valued type $S_b$ such that $(b,c]$ satisfies Equation <a href="#ctr:record" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ctr:record">[ctr:record]</a> for the specified new test family and tolerance $\delta$. Its exponents lie in $[m-\eta,m]$. If the parent cut is present, its exponent belongs to $(0,(1-\eta)m]$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-ct-new-record"><summary>Proof of stmt-ct-new-record</summary>

*Proof.* For two distinct natural first-level samples, average over the first $I$ sites the product of their $\phi_j(S_1)$ values. Conditional on their globals and Gaussian fields, the site’s root variable is shared between the two samples, and its first-level variables are independent. The conditional mean is exactly $Q_j$ from <a href="#ct:features" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ct:features">[ct:features]</a>. Therefore <a id="ct:new-empirical-bound"></a>$$\label{ct:new-empirical-bound}
       \mathbb E\left|\frac1I\sum_{i=1}^I
              \phi_j(S_1^{1,i})\phi_j(S_1^{2,i})-Q_j(1,2)
          \right|^2\le I^{-1}.$$ For each fixed $I$ this inequality passes by continuity to the natural-order limit array. Along dyadic $I$, it gives almost-sure convergence of the empirical average to $Q_j$, simultaneously for all pairs and tests.

After reindexing, take two sample positions agreeing through $b$ and diverging immediately below $b$. If the parent is present, they are in the same parent cluster; otherwise $\mathbb P(Q<q)=0$. If the child is present, they lie in distinct child clusters; otherwise $\mathbb P(Q<r)=1$. Consequently their natural pair overlap satisfies $q\le Q<r$ almost surely in all cases. By <a href="#ct:quantile-band" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ct:quantile-band">[ct:quantile-band]</a> and Lemma <a href="#ct:ordering" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ct:ordering">20</a>, the corresponding $Q_j$ belongs to a fixed closed interval $I_j$ of length at most $J_f\gamma$. The empirical characterization just proved shows that this interval condition is a property of the reindexed decorations alone; we do not need a separate representation of the kernels $Q_j$.

<a id="ct:new-vectors"></a>
In the product-tree representation, the site empirical limit is the inner product in $L^2(\,\mathrm du_{0:b})$ of the two vectors $$\label{ct:new-vectors}
          A_j(u_{0:b})=
                     \int\phi_j(S_c)\,\,\mathrm du_{b+1:c}.$$ Conditional on the ancestor globals through $b$, these are independent copies as their continuation globals vary. Their inner product belongs to $I_j$ almost surely. The second assertion of Lemma <a href="#ct:support" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ct:support">24</a> shows that the conditional support of $A_j$ has diameter at most $\sqrt{2J_f\gamma}$.

<a id="ct:new-type"></a>
Define, for each ancestor global and site history, $$\label{ct:new-type}
 S_b(z_{0:b},u_{0:b})
       =\operatorname{Law}_{z_{b+1:c},u_{b+1:c}}(S_c).$$ All continuation variables in this formula have their current independent uniform laws. This is a measurable probability kernel on the type space of $S_c$, hence has exactly the required next type space. By Fubini’s theorem, $S_b(\phi_j)$ is the conditional continuation-global barycenter of the vector $A_j$ in <a href="#ct:new-vectors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ct:new-vectors">[ct:new-vectors]</a>. The support diameter bound gives $$\int\left|
           \int\phi_j(S_c)\,\,\mathrm du_{b+1:c}-S_b(\phi_j)
      \right|^2\,\mathrm du_{0:b}\le 2J_f\gamma$$ for almost every complete global path. Choose $0<\gamma\le\delta^2/(2J_f)$ to obtain the new record.

It remains to check the exponent band. The only possible intermediate level between $b$ and $c$ is the child cut, whose exponent is $mx_c$. By <a href="#ct:quantile-band" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ct:quantile-band">[ct:quantile-band]</a>, $x_c\ge1-\eta$, so $m-mx_c\le m\eta\le\eta$. The end exponent is $m$. If the parent is present, its exponent satisfies $0<mx_p\le(1-\eta)m$, as claimed.

The type in <a href="#ct:new-type" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ct:new-type">[ct:new-type]</a> is defined once, at this transition. In subsequent posterior changes it is retained as the same measurable function; it is not redefined by averaging under the changed global law. The pathwise record just proved then survives by absolute continuity, exactly as do the old records. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-ct-new-record-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-ct-new-record</summary>

*Completion of the proof of Lemma <a href="#ct:transition" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ct:transition">17</a>.* The compactness and representation construction inserts at most two levels. Lemma <a href="#ct:endpoint" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ct:endpoint">23</a> supplies the limiting expected endpoint values and $G_k/k\le U$. Lemma <a href="#ct:old-records" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ct:old-records">25</a> preserves every old record, while their exponent-band inequalities survive ordinary limits. Lemma <a href="#ct:new-record" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ct:new-record">26</a> supplies the new processed block. If there is no parent cut it begins at the root and processing is complete. Otherwise the hierarchy is active, its first exponent is $mx_p>0$, and this is at most $(1-\eta)m$. All records hold for almost every complete global path. These constructions were made for the fixed output horizon $k$; repeating them for any other fixed horizon proves the asserted quantifiers. All nonroot shared globals remain in this output. The next section removes them by a separate approximation argument. ◻

</details>

<a id="cc:section"></a>
### Removing the shared variables and completing the trials

The transition in Lemma <a href="#ct:transition" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ct:transition">17</a> keeps the shared variables and preserves the conditional records. We now use it finitely many times, then replace the nonroot shared variables by independent site sampling. The order matters: the replacement estimate is for one packet, so we first reduce the trial to a one-packet horizon. The final step approximates the resulting conditional sampling laws by finite rational tables.

Panchenko’s theorem for finite replica symmetry breaking (Panchenko 2016, arXiv version, Theorem 2) <a id="pm-cite-40"></a>[[Panchenko2015FRSB](#ref-Panchenko2015FRSB)] removes common nonroot coordinates while retaining common root data, under spin-tested Ghirlanda–Guerra identities, cavity equations for a modified Hamiltonian, and an overlap law with finitely many exact values. Here the overlap cuts partition a possibly continuous distribution. The recorded conditional laws and the quantitative estimates below provide the approximation needed to construct finite rational trials.

Panchenko’s spin-distribution formula optimizes over a wider invariant distributional class in its stated even-arity setting; its shared-coordinate obstruction is explicit after Lemma 6 (Panchenko 2013a, arXiv version, Theorem 2 and the Remark after Lemma 6) <a id="pm-cite-41"></a>[[Panchenko2013SD](#ref-Panchenko2013SD)].

<a id="pm-h-29"></a>
#### From recorded tests to probability kernels

Write $W_1$ for the Wasserstein metric associated with the metric on a compact type space. Every iterated type space $K^{(j)}$ in the structural record <a href="#ctr:record" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ctr:record">[ctr:record]</a> is compact and has diameter at most one. Write $z=(z_0,\ldots,z_d)$ for a complete global path.

<a id="stmt-cc-site-law"></a>
<div id="cc:site-law" class="lemma">

<a id="cc:net-bound"></a>
<a id="cc:delta-choice"></a>
<a id="cc:site-law-distance"></a>
**Lemma 27** (Finite tests for the site law). *Given $e_0>0$ and a finite nonempty list of type spaces, one can choose finite nonempty collections $\Phi_j$ of $[0,1]$-valued $1$-Lipschitz functions such that, for probability measures $\nu,S$ on $K^{(j)}$, $$\label{cc:net-bound}
 W_1(\nu,S)
 \le\max_{\phi\in\Phi_j}|\nu(\phi)-S(\phi)|+e_0/2.$$ If the structural record holds with $$\label{cc:delta-choice}
 \bigl(\max_j|\Phi_j|\bigr)\delta\le e_0/2,$$ then, for each processed block $(b,c]$ and almost every fixed global path, $$\label{cc:site-law-distance}
 \int W_1\bigl(\nu_{z,u_{0:b}},S_b(z_{0:b},u_{0:b})\bigr)
               \,\,\mathrm du_{0:b}\le e_0.$$ Here $\nu_{z,u_{0:b}}$ is the conditional law of $S_c$ obtained by integrating only the fresh site coordinates $u_{b+1:c}$, with all the global coordinates fixed.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cc-site-law"><summary>Proof of stmt-cc-site-law</summary>

*Proof.* Kantorovich–Rubinstein duality expresses $W_1$ as the supremum of integral differences over $1$-Lipschitz functions. Constants cancel from such differences; since the space has diameter at most one, each test can be shifted into $[0,1]$. The class of these bounded Lipschitz functions is compact in the uniform norm. A finite $e_0/4$-net gives Equation <a href="#cc:net-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cc:net-bound">[cc:net-bound]</a>, since replacing a test by a net point changes the difference of its two integrals by at most $e_0/2$.

For a fixed test, the structural record and Cauchy–Schwarz give $$\int |\nu_{z,u_{0:b}}(\phi)-S_b(z_{0:b},u_{0:b})(\phi)|
                 \,\,\mathrm du_{0:b}\le\delta.$$ Bound the maximum over tests by their sum, integrate Equation <a href="#cc:net-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cc:net-bound">[cc:net-bound]</a>, and use Equation <a href="#cc:delta-choice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cc:delta-choice">[cc:delta-choice]</a>. This proves Equation <a href="#cc:site-law-distance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cc:site-law-distance">[cc:site-law-distance]</a> with its almost-everywhere global quantifier intact. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-30"></a>
#### Finite stopping

<a id="stmt-cc-terminal"></a>
<div id="cc:terminal" class="proposition">

<a id="cc:stopping-depth"></a>
**Proposition 28** (A terminal one-packet trial). *Fix $U>P(a,\beta)$, $0<\eta,m_0<1$, and an integer $J$ such that $$\label{cc:stopping-depth}
 (1-\eta)^J<m_0.$$ Choose in advance finite nonempty test sets on $K^{(j)}$, $0\le j\le J$, and a structural tolerance $\delta>0$. For every $\rho>0$ there is a trial with $$G_1\le U+\rho,$$ at most $J$ processed blocks, and depth at most $2J+1$, which satisfies the chosen structural records and has one of the following forms:*

1.  *the processed blocks start at the root boundary $0$;*

2.  *the processed blocks start at boundary $1$, and the remaining first exponent satisfies $0<m<m_0$.*

*Every processed block has exponent width at most $\eta$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cc-terminal"><summary>Proof of stmt-cc-terminal</summary>

*Proof.* Lemma <a href="#ctr:reservoir" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ctr:reservoir">14</a> supplies trials for arbitrarily large integer horizons $N$, initially of depth one and first exponent $1$, with $G_N/N\le U$. There are initially no processed blocks. A trial is terminal if its blocks start at the root or if its first unprocessed exponent is less than $m_0$.

Suppose a sequence at a given stage contains no terminal trial. All its first exponents are then at least $m_0$. Depth has increased by at most two at each preceding transition, so there are only finitely many possible patterns of block boundaries. Pass to an unbounded horizon subsequence with a common pattern, and then to a subsequence on which the exponents converge. Lemma <a href="#ct:transition" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ct:transition">17</a>, with its fixed tests and tolerance, produces for each fixed positive integer $k$ an output trial satisfying $G_k/k\le U$. It preserves the old records and adds one processed block. Either the output is terminal, or its new first exponent is at most $(1-\eta)$ times the limiting old first exponent.

This use of the transition involves nested limits, not a uniform limit in $k$. First fix $k$; then take the input horizon to infinity as required by the transition; finally retain an output for that $k$. Doing this separately for $k=1,2,\ldots$ gives the next unbounded horizon sequence if no terminal output occurs. After $j$ transitions every still-active output has first exponent at most $(1-\eta)^j$. Equation <a href="#cc:stopping-depth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cc:stopping-depth">[cc:stopping-depth]</a> therefore forces termination after at most $J$ transitions. The resulting terminal trial has some finite horizon, at most $J$ blocks, and depth at most $2J+1$.

Reduce its horizon to one using the packet telescope of Lemma <a href="#ctr:posterior" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ctr:posterior">15</a>, without a Gaussian perturbation. There is no remainder when the blocks have size one. The sum of the averaged one-packet posterior differences is the original $G_k$, so one step, and then base root data outside the conditional null sets, have posterior value at most $U+\rho$. This selection is made before fresh packet data are sampled. The conditional laws generated by the posterior densities remain equivalent to the old laws, and the old site-law functions are kept rather than recomputed. Hence all the almost-everywhere records persist. Neither the exponents nor the terminal boundary changes. This gives the asserted one-packet trial. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-31"></a>
#### Removing shared variables after stopping

<a id="stmt-cc-kernel-collapse"></a>
<div id="cc:kernel-collapse" class="lemma">

<a id="cc:collapse-error"></a>
**Lemma 29** (Collapse by the recorded kernels). *Consider a terminal trial from Proposition <a href="#cc:terminal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cc:terminal">28</a>, whose records imply Equation <a href="#cc:site-law-distance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cc:site-law-distance">[cc:site-law-distance]</a>. Fix a count cutoff $D_0\ge1$ and put $$C=\log2+\beta D_0,\qquad M=3D_0,
 \qquad
 R=JM e^\beta e^{2C(2J+2)}e_0.$$ There is a site-only trial, with weakly increasing exponents in $(0,1]$, such that $$\label{cc:collapse-error}
 |G_1^{\mathrm{site}}-G_1|
 \le 2K(C)\eta+2R+C^2e^{2C}m_0+2\tau_A+2\tau_B,$$ where $$\tau_A=\mathbb E[(\log2+\beta D_A)\mathbf 1_{\{D_A>D_0\}}],\qquad
 \tau_B=\mathbb E[(\log2+\beta D_B)\mathbf 1_{\{D_B>D_0\}}],$$ with $D_A\sim\operatorname{Poi}(3a)$ and $D_B\sim\operatorname{Poi}(2a)$. In the root-terminal case the term containing $m_0$ is unnecessary. The new message kernels are independent of the fresh packet counts and signs.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cc-kernel-collapse"><summary>Proof of stmt-cc-kernel-collapse</summary>

*Proof.* We first condition on either endpoint’s fresh count and sign data with $D\le D_0$. Its loss $X$ is bounded in absolute value by $C$, and it uses at most $M$ distinct reservoir occurrences. Simultaneously replace all exponents in each processed block $(b,c]$ by $t_c$. The resulting vector is still nondecreasing and is within $\eta$ of the original. Lemma <a href="#cs:sensitivity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cs:sensitivity">16</a> bounds the change by $K(C)\eta$. A block with constant exponent is one conditional $T_{t_c}$ operation over all its coordinates, by the tower property.

<a id="cc:kernel-recursion"></a>
Define deterministic functions of vectors of single-site types, one coordinate for each reservoir occurrence, working upwards through the block boundaries. At the last boundary put $F_d(\mathbf S_d)=X$. For a block $(b,c]$ put $$\label{cc:kernel-recursion}
 F_b(\mathbf s)=\frac1{t_c}\log\int
       \exp\bigl(t_cF_c(\mathbf u)\bigr)\prod_i s_i(\,\mathrm du_i).$$ These functions depend on the fixed count and sign data, but the probability kernels $S_b$ do not. The functions $F_b$ are bounded in absolute value by $C$.

Use the sum metric on each finite product of type spaces. The terminal loss is $e^\beta$-Lipschitz: a clause factor is bounded below by $1-w=e^{-\beta}$, the product of at most three $[0,1]$ messages changes by at most the sum of their changes, and the log-sum-exp for the two branches of an A packet preserves the largest branchwise log error. Thus we may take $L_d=e^\beta$ as a Lipschitz constant for $F_d$. Coordinatewise couplings in Equation <a href="#cc:kernel-recursion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cc:kernel-recursion">[cc:kernel-recursion]</a> give <a id="cc:lipschitz-recursion"></a>$$\label{cc:lipschitz-recursion}
 L_b\le e^{2C}L_c.$$ Indeed $v\mapsto e^{t_cv}$ has Lipschitz constant at most $t_ce^C$ on $[-C,C]$, and the divided logarithm on the possible integral values has Lipschitz constant at most $e^C/t_c$. The same two bounds show that a mean absolute error in an inside value propagates through a block by at most the factor $e^{2C}$.

<a id="cc:one-block-error"></a>
Let $Y_b$ denote the actual effective value at boundary $b$ after flattening the processed blocks’ exponents. Thus $Y_d=X$, and across a block $(b,c]$ the value $Y_b$ is the conditional $T_{t_c}$ average of $Y_c$ over the block’s global and site coordinates. Define $$E_b=\mathbb E_{\mathrm{base}}
       \bigl|Y_b-F_b(\mathbf S_b)\bigr|.$$ Here the expectation uses the current trial law on the global history and all occurrences’ fresh site histories through $b$, before any exponential reweighting by this fresh packet. This law already includes any old-packet posterior used to obtain the trial; the packet count and signs remain fixed. In particular $E_d=0$. We claim that $$E_b\le e^{2C}E_c+e^{2C}L_cMe_0.
 \label{cc:one-block-error}$$

First replace $Y_c$ inside the block average by $F_c(\mathbf S_c)$. The mean-error propagation bound above gives the first term of Equation <a href="#cc:one-block-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cc:one-block-error">[cc:one-block-error]</a>. To bound the remaining error, fix the ancestor histories through $b$ and the continuation globals through $c$. Under this conditioning, the occurrences’ fresh site continuations are independent; write $\nu_i$ for the resulting law of $S_c^i$. A product of Wasserstein couplings and the Lipschitz constant of $F_c$ give $$\left|\int e^{t_cF_c(\mathbf u)}\prod_i\nu_i(\,\mathrm du_i)
       -\int e^{t_cF_c(\mathbf u)}\prod_iS_b^i(\,\mathrm du_i)\right|
 \le t_ce^C L_c\sum_i W_1(\nu_i,S_b^i).$$ The second integral depends only on the ancestor histories. Average this inequality over the continuation globals, apply the $e^C/t_c$ Lipschitz bound for the divided logarithm, and then average over the ancestor histories. For almost every fixed global path, Equation <a href="#cc:site-law-distance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cc:site-law-distance">[cc:site-law-distance]</a> bounds the integral over each occurrence’s site ancestors by $e_0$. The continuation-global law is independent of these fresh site generators, including when the trial was obtained by a posterior change of law. Fubini’s theorem therefore bounds the final average by $e^{2C}L_cMe_0$ and proves Equation <a href="#cc:one-block-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cc:one-block-error">[cc:one-block-error]</a>.

There are at most $J$ processed blocks, and $L_c\le e^{2CJ}e^\beta$ at every boundary. Iterating Equation <a href="#cc:one-block-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cc:one-block-error">[cc:one-block-error]</a> from $E_d=0$ bounds their accumulated error. If the starting boundary is $1$, propagating once through the remaining first-level transform adds at most one factor $e^{2C}$. In either case the error is at most $$JM e^\beta e^{2C(2J+2)}e_0=R.$$

If the processed blocks begin at the root, the resulting recursion already describes a site-only trial. Retain the common root $z_0$ and sample each occurrence’s initial type $S_0(z_0,u_0^i)$ using its own fresh root-site uniform. At one level of exponent $t_c$ for each old block, draw each site’s next type independently from its preceding type, and continue to the message pair. Probability kernels on these compact metric spaces can be sampled by measurable functions of fresh uniform variables. This construction evaluates Equation <a href="#cc:kernel-recursion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cc:kernel-recursion">[cc:kernel-recursion]</a>.

If the blocks begin at boundary $1$, there is still the operation $T_mF_1(\mathbf S_1)$, where $0<m<m_0$. Conditional on the root data, factor this operation as $$T_m^{z_1}T_m^{\mathrm{sites}}F_1(\mathbf S_1).$$ The inside quantity is in $[-C,C]$. By Equation <a href="#cs:small-exponent" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cs:small-exponent">[cs:small-exponent]</a>, replacing the outside $T_m^{z_1}$ by ordinary expectation changes its value by at most $\tfrac12 C^2e^{2C}m_0$. Move $z_1$, with this ordinary sampling law, into the root global data. Retain the level-$1$ site transform and the independent block draws just constructed. This is again site-only. Its kernels were constructed from the recorded types alone, not from any fresh count or sign data.

For counts greater than $D_0$, both the old and new endpoint transforms are bounded in absolute value by $\log2+\beta D$, regardless of their kernels or exponents. Their contribution to the difference is thus at most twice the corresponding tail expectation. Summing the bounded-count estimates for the two endpoints proves Equation <a href="#cc:collapse-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cc:collapse-error">[cc:collapse-error]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-32"></a>
#### Rational finite descriptions

<a id="stmt-cc-rational"></a>
<div id="cc:rational" class="theorem">

<a id="cc:finite-infimum"></a>
**Theorem 30** (Finite rational completeness). *<span id="cc:complete" label="cc:complete"></span> For every $a>0$ and finite $\beta>0$, $$\label{cc:finite-infimum}
 P(a,\beta)=\inf_{\mathcal Q}G_1,$$ where $\mathcal Q$ consists of site-only trials of finite depth whose exponents are strictly increasing rationals in $(0,1)$ and whose message pair is a rational-valued step function on a finite rational rectangular grid in its root and site uniform variables. In particular, this is a countable class with an effective enumeration.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cc-rational"><summary>Proof of stmt-cc-rational</summary>

*Proof.* The upper bound of Proposition <a href="#ctr:upper" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ctr:upper">12</a> gives $P(a,\beta)\le G_1$ for every member of $\mathcal Q$. We prove the reverse inequality by giving the order of all approximation choices.

Fix $\epsilon>0$ and put $U=P(a,\beta)+\epsilon$. First choose a count cutoff $D_0\ge1$ with $\tau_A,\tau_B\le\epsilon$, and set $C=\log2+\beta D_0$. Choose $\eta,m_0\in(0,1)$ so that $$K(C)\eta\le\epsilon,\qquad
 \tfrac12 C^2e^{2C}m_0\le\epsilon.$$ Next choose $J$ satisfying Equation <a href="#cc:stopping-depth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cc:stopping-depth">[cc:stopping-depth]</a>, and only then choose $e_0>0$ so small that $$J(3D_0)e^\beta e^{2C(2J+2)}e_0\le\epsilon.$$ Choose the finite test sets on all type spaces through $K^{(J)}$ by Lemma <a href="#cc:site-law" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cc:site-law">27</a>, and finally choose $\delta$ satisfying Equation <a href="#cc:delta-choice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cc:delta-choice">[cc:delta-choice]</a>. In particular, neither the test precision nor the allowed number of transitions is chosen in response to the eventual hierarchy depth. The overlap widths used inside each transition are chosen only after these test sets and $\delta$, as in Lemma <a href="#ct:transition" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ct:transition">17</a>.

Proposition <a href="#cc:terminal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cc:terminal">28</a>, with $\rho=\epsilon$, gives a terminal one-packet trial of value at most $P+2\epsilon$. Lemma <a href="#cc:kernel-collapse" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cc:kernel-collapse">29</a> turns it into a site-only trial whose value is at most $P+12\epsilon$: the exponent changes contribute at most $2\epsilon$, the kernel replacements at most $2\epsilon$, the possible small-exponent replacement at most $2\epsilon$, and the two tails at most $4\epsilon$.

It remains to obtain a finite rational description. Approximate the finite weakly increasing exponent vector by strictly increasing rational vectors in $(0,1)$. This is possible even if some original exponents coincide or equal $1$. For fixed count data, Lemma <a href="#cs:sensitivity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cs:sensitivity">16</a> gives convergence of the transform values. The packet bound of Equation <a href="#ctr:packet-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ctr:packet-bound">[ctr:packet-bound]</a>, uniform over the approximations, then gives convergence after averaging the Poisson counts and signs.

Encode the root randomness by a single uniform coordinate. After the independent conditional type samplings have likewise been encoded by uniform coordinates, the message pair is a measurable function of finitely many independent uniforms: the root global, the root site coordinate, and the finitely many site-level coordinates. Approximate this pair in $L^1$ by rational-valued step functions on finite dyadic rectangular grids. For example, conditional expectations on the successive dyadic grids converge in $L^1$, and subsequent rational quantization preserves the range $[0,1]^2$ and the convergence. The shared root coordinate is retained as shared; no independence across sites is introduced there.

For fixed counts and signs, the leaf Lipschitz estimate in the proof of Lemma <a href="#cc:kernel-collapse" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cc:kernel-collapse">29</a> bounds the mean leaf error by $e^\beta$ times the sum of the $L^1$ message errors over the finitely many occurrences. The mean-error propagation bound through the fixed finite hierarchy proves convergence of both endpoint values. Once more, the root linear-in-count bound makes the Poisson tails uniform, so their fully averaged $G_1$ values converge. Thus a member of $\mathcal Q$ has value at most $P+13\epsilon$.

Since $\epsilon$ is arbitrary, Equation <a href="#cc:finite-infimum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cc:finite-infimum">[cc:finite-infimum]</a> follows. Finite depths, finite grids, rational exponents, and finite rational tables all have finite encodings and can be enumerated by a single ordinary algorithm. These are the trials used in the certificate search; none of the compactness limits or tolerance choices in the existence proof is input to that search. The larger class of strict measurable site-only trials contains $\mathcal Q$ and obeys the same upper bound, so its infimum also equals $P(a,\beta)$. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:assembly"></a>
### Completing the computation

We now have the two certificate families described in Section <a href="#sec:certificates" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:certificates">2</a>. Let $\alpha$ be the threshold constructed in Theorem <a href="#cp:threshold" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cp:threshold">6</a>. Proposition <a href="#cp:finite-computation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cp:finite-computation">7</a> gives a computable rational sequence $(\ell_n)$ with $\ell_n<\alpha$ and $\ell_n\to\alpha$. Its underlying finite-size bound is the exact one-sided estimate $$\frac{f_n}{n}-2^{67}n^{-1/6}\le\alpha.$$ For the upper certificates, a rational trial is the finite site-only description of Section <a href="#ctr:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ctr:section">5</a>: it has strictly increasing rational exponents in $(0,1)$ and a rational-valued message pair on a finite rational rectangular grid of the root and site uniform variables.

<a id="stmt-assembly-upper"></a>
<div id="assembly:upper" class="lemma">

**Lemma 31** (Upper certificates). *For positive rational $a$, positive integer $\beta$, and a rational trial $Q$, the inequality $G_1(a,\beta;Q)<0$ certifies $\alpha\le a$. Every rational $a>\alpha$ has such a certificate. The class of certified densities is effectively enumerable.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-assembly-upper"><summary>Proof of stmt-assembly-upper</summary>

*Proof.* The trial bound of Proposition <a href="#ctr:upper" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ctr:upper">12</a> gives $P(a,\beta)\le G_1(a,\beta;Q)$. If $a<\alpha$, the pressure exists by Lemma <a href="#ctr:pressure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ctr:pressure">11</a> and is nonnegative by Corollary <a href="#cp:soft-signs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cp:soft-signs">9</a>. Thus a negative trial is impossible below $\alpha$ and certifies the non-strict inequality $\alpha\le a$. Nothing about the pressure at equality is needed.

If $a>\alpha$, Corollary <a href="#cp:soft-signs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cp:soft-signs">9</a> supplies a positive integer $\beta$ for which $P(a,\beta)<0$. Theorem <a href="#cc:complete" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cc:complete">[cc:complete]</a> says that $P(a,\beta)$ is the infimum of the rational trial values. A rational trial therefore has negative value. Its finite description is eventually enumerated, and Lemma <a href="#ctr:evaluation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ctr:evaluation">10</a> eventually gives a rational interval for its value with negative upper endpoint. Enumerating positive rational densities, positive integer penalties, finite descriptions and accuracy indices gives the asserted enumeration. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-assembly-upper-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-assembly-upper</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* Theorem <a href="#cp:threshold" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cp:threshold">6</a> proves the strict-side probability limits for the proper three-clause model in the introduction, with $\alpha\in[1/200,10]$. Set $\alpha_3=\alpha$. This also identifies the constant with the same-model $k=3$ threshold in (OpenAI 2026a, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-42"></a>[[FixedClauseThreshold](#ref-FixedClauseThreshold)] by the uniqueness argument in Section <a href="#sec:proof-map" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:proof-map">3</a>.

Use the sequence from Proposition <a href="#cp:finite-computation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cp:finite-computation">7</a> as the lower sequence in Lemma <a href="#cert:search" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:search">2</a>, and use $G=G_1$ on the rational trial class. This class is effectively enumerable by its finite encoding, and Lemma <a href="#ctr:evaluation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ctr:evaluation">10</a> proves uniform computability of its values. Lemma <a href="#assembly:upper" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="assembly:upper">31</a> proves both sign hypotheses of the search lemma. Since $\alpha\in[0,20]$, all its assumptions hold. The resulting single finite machine halts on every unary input $1^r$ and returns the required binary-encoded rational within $2^{-r}$.

The compactness subsequences, conditional laws, Gaussian parameters and approximation tolerances used to prove completeness are not inputs to the machine. They establish the existence of finite witnesses, which the search finds through its fixed enumeration. The running time of this fixed machine on $1^r$ is itself a total computable function of $r$, obtained by simulating the halting computation; the proof supplies no efficiency estimate for that function. ◻

</details>

      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[>=Latex,x=1cm,y=1cm,every node/.style={font=\\small}]\n \\draw[->] (0,0.45)--(0,-4.45);\n \\node[anchor=east] at (-.2,.35) {root};\n \\foreach \\y/\\label in {-.65/{parent cut},-1.85/{child cut},-3.05/{old sample level},-4.1/{old descendants}} {\n  \\draw (-.08,\\y)--(.08,\\y);\n  \\node[anchor=east] at (-.2,\\y) {\\label};\n }\n \\node[anchor=west] at (.25,-.65) {$m x_p\\le(1-\\eta)m$};\n \\node[anchor=west] at (.25,-1.85) {$m x_c\\ge(1-\\eta)m$};\n \\node[anchor=west] at (.25,-3.05) {$m$};\n \\node[anchor=west] at (.25,-4.1) {$t_2,\\ldots,t_d$};\n \\draw[decorate,decoration={brace,amplitude=5pt}] (4.3,-1.65)--(4.3,-3.25);\n \\node[anchor=west,align=left,text width=3.1cm] at (4.6,-2.45)\n  {new processed block:\\\\exponents lie in $[m-\\eta,m]$};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "A vertical axis with a downward-pointing arrow at the bottom displaying a hierarchical progression of levels. On the left side of the axis, tick marks are labeled from top to bottom as: \"root\", \"parent cut\", \"child cut\", \"old sample level\", and \"old descendants\" (positioned by the bottom arrow). On the right side of the axis, values and expressions are given: next to \"parent cut\" is $mx_p \\le (1 - \\eta)m$; next to \"child cut\" is $mx_c \\ge (1 - \\eta)m$; next to \"old sample level\" is $m$; next to \"old descendants\" is $t_2, \\dots, t_d$. A large right-facing curly bracket spans from the \"child cut\" level down to the \"old sample level\", with an annotation to its right reading: \"new processed block: exponents lie in $[m - \\eta, m]$\"."
